# AI-Enhanced Cloud-Edge-Terminal Collaborative Network: Survey, Applications, and Future Directions

Huixian G[u](https://orcid.org/0000-0003-4660-6465) , *Graduate St[ude](https://orcid.org/0000-0001-8457-6477)nt Member, IEEE*, Liqiang Zhao [,](https://orcid.org/0000-0002-3374-6066) *[M](https://orcid.org/0000-0001-6316-8415)ember, IEEE*, Zhu Han [,](https://orcid.org/0000-0002-6606-5822) *Fellow, IEEE*, Gan Zheng , *Fellow, IEEE*, and Shenghui Song , *Senior Member, IEEE*

*Abstract***—The cloud-edge-terminal collaborative network (CETCN) is considered as a novel paradigm for emerging applications owing to its huge potential in providing low-latency and ultra-reliable computing services. However, achieving such benefits is very challenging due to the heterogeneous computing power of terminal devices and the complex environment faced by the CETCN. In particular, the high-dimensional and dynamic environment states cause difficulties for the CETCN to make efficient decisions in terms of task offloading, collaborative caching and mobility management. To this end, artificial intelligence (AI), especially deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has been proven effective in solving sequential decision-making problems in various domains, and offers a promising solution for the above-mentioned issues due to several reasons. Firstly, accurate modelling of the CETCN, which is difficult to obtain for realworld applications, is not required for the DRL-based method. Secondly, DRL can effectively respond to high-dimensional and dynamic tasks through iterative interactions with the environment. Thirdly, due to the complexity of tasks and the differences**

**in resource supply among different vendors, collaboration is required between different vendors to complete tasks. The multiagent DRL (MADRL) methods are very effective in solving collaborative tasks, where the collaborative tasks can be jointly completed by cloud, edge and terminal devices which provided by different vendors. This survey provides a comprehensive overview regarding the applications of DRL and MADRL in the context of CETCN. The first part of this survey provides a depth overview of the key concepts of the CETCN and the mathematical underpinnings of both DRL and MADRL. Then, we highlight the applications of RL algorithms in solving various challenges within CETCN, such as task offloading, resource allocation, caching and mobility management. In addition, we extend discussion to explore how DRL and MADRL are making inroads into emerging CETCN scenarios like intelligent transportation system (ITS), the industrial Internet of Things (IIoT), smart health and digital agriculture. Furthermore, security considerations related to the application of DRL within CETCN are addressed, along with an overview of existing standards that pertain to edge intelligence. Finally, we list several lessons learned in this evolving field and outline future research opportunities and challenges that are critical for the development of the CETCN. We hope this survey will attract more researchers to investigate scalable and decentralized AI algorithms for the design of CETCN.**

*Index Terms***—Cloud-edge-terminal collaborative network (CETCN), single-agent reinforcement learning (SARL), multiagent reinforcement learning (MARL), network architecture, task offloading, resource allocation, collaborative caching, mobility management, applications, security, standards.**

## <span id="page-0-7"></span><span id="page-0-6"></span><span id="page-0-5"></span><span id="page-0-3"></span><span id="page-0-2"></span><span id="page-0-1"></span>I. INTRODUCTION

<span id="page-0-8"></span><span id="page-0-4"></span><span id="page-0-0"></span>**I** N RECENT years, in the fields of industry [\[1\]](#page-52-0), transportation [\[2\]](#page-52-1), Internet of Things (IoT) [\[3\]](#page-52-2) and network security [\[4\]](#page-52-3), intelligent cloud-edge-terminal collaborative network (CETCN) attracts more and more researchers' attention since it can complete complex networking operations under limited human participation. The advantages of intelligent cloud-edge-terminal collaborative networks not only can reduce network deployment costs [\[5\]](#page-52-4), but also can improve network reliability and efficiency [\[6\]](#page-52-5), [\[7\]](#page-52-6). Meanwhile, the rapid growth of the Internet has brought up new demands for the multi-dimensional performance indexes, such as extremely low latency requirements, more reasonable resource allocation, uninterrupted service demand, and reliable security guarantees [\[8\]](#page-52-7) and traditional centralized cloud computing architecture can no longer fully meet these requirements [\[9\]](#page-52-8), [\[10\]](#page-52-9). Therefore, the capabilities of edge computing nodes (base stations, wireless access point, etc.) and terminal devices (laptops, intelligent wearable devices, vehicles, etc.) should be utilized to support emerging applications [\[11\]](#page-52-10).

<span id="page-1-1"></span>Reasonable allocation of network resources is an issue that cannot be ignored to perform task offloading due to the inherent shortcomings of edge computing nodes and terminal devices, such as limited resources (computing and storage resources) and mobile characteristics of terminal devices. Traditionally, many computation-intensive applications are offloaded to cloud servers with powerful computing capabilities [\[12\]](#page-52-11), [\[13\]](#page-52-12), [\[14\]](#page-52-13). However, long distance transmission consumes a large amount of bandwidth resources of backhaul networks and results in a substantial increase in energy consumption and delay. For example, Google Docs can achieve effective and real-time online collaborative document sharing and editing, and such services can be executed in cloud servers, users only need to send their requirements to the cloud server and then computation resource will be allocated to process the tasks automatically. However, the long distance between users and cloud servers cannot be ignored when processing some real-time applications, such as remote surgery and smart transportation, which have also been applied in practical system, millisecond level delays could cause serious accidents. Fortunately, with the aid of edge computing nodes and terminal devices, some tasks can be offloaded to the idle edge servers or terminal devices to execute, and in this manner, the bandwidth of backbone network, energy consumption and delay can be reduced when processing real-time and delay sensitive applications. By offloading part of tasks to the network edge [\[15\]](#page-52-14), [\[16\]](#page-52-15), [\[17\]](#page-52-16), the problem of resource occupancy and energy consumption can be successfully solved [\[18\]](#page-52-17).

<span id="page-1-5"></span><span id="page-1-2"></span>In CETCNs, resource allocation is another key issue concurrence with task offloading. Through reasonable resource allocation, edge devices can better handle various tasks. Many researchers have proposed various methods to solve multidimensional resource allocation problems in the cloud-edge collaborative environment, including methods based on convex optimization [\[19\]](#page-52-18), [\[20\]](#page-52-19), [\[21\]](#page-52-20), game theory [\[22\]](#page-52-21), [\[23\]](#page-52-22), [\[24\]](#page-52-23) and artificial intelligence [\[25\]](#page-52-24), [\[26\]](#page-52-25), [\[27\]](#page-52-26). Generally, resource allocation refers to the effective allocation of limited resources to tasks through some methods, so as to achieve users' objectives. Specifically, for the CETCN, the key terms of resource allocation can be divided as follows: 1) Resources: there are various available resources at the edge of the network, but these resources are often precious and limited, such as computing resources, storage resources and communication resources, 2) Tasks: Tasks often are generated from users, such as Ultra High Definition (UHD) broadcast and video processing. 3) Objectives: Different task have different objectives during task processing. For example, UHD live broadcast often requires extremely low transmission delay, and video processing often requires abundant computing resources. The objectives are often refers to various performance indexes. However, tens of billions of heterogeneous devices may generate tens of billions of heterogeneous tasks, it is difficult to allocate reasonable resources to these tasks through traditional methods due to its NP-hard property.

Caching content and service at edge devices can reduce the delay and increase users' Quality of Service (QoS). In addition <span id="page-1-8"></span><span id="page-1-7"></span><span id="page-1-0"></span>to offloading the generated task to the edge device, users can also retrieve popular documents from edge devices [\[28\]](#page-53-0), [\[29\]](#page-53-1). Extensive research has indicated that a large part of traffic on the Internet came from the repeated viewing and downloading of popular content [\[30\]](#page-53-2), [\[31\]](#page-53-3). For content providers, such as YouTube and TikTok, they need to cache the hot content in edge devices to maximize their revenue. For hardware device providers, such as Google Cloud Platform (GCP), they need to attract content providers to rent or buy hardware equipments by setting a reasonable price of their equipments. For users, they would like to be able to get the content they need at minimal cost. On the one hand, duplicate content acquisition puts huge traffic pressure on the network. On the other hand, various applications and service caching have additional computation requirements and also generate delay and energy consumption. When users request application service or content from the cloud servers, users may suffer unbearable delays due to the long distance transmission, and operators will consume more unnecessary bandwidth resources of the core network and the content providers will spend more money on purchasing cloud servers. At the same time, if users request content or service from the edge layer, the limited caching capacity should be considered when designing appropriate caching strategy. In this context, content caching and service caching based on the CETCN can reduce latency, save cost and take advantage of the rich bandwidth resources at the edge. Due to the heterogeneously distribution and the limitation of edge resources, the content and the service placement problem should be reviewed comprehensively in the CETCN.

<span id="page-1-9"></span><span id="page-1-3"></span>Mobility management is another research topic that should not be ignored in the CETCN. User's mobility in realworld scenarios makes task offloading, resource allocation, and caching decision problems more difficult [\[32\]](#page-53-4). The obstacle mainly comes from the uncertainty and dynamics of the channel due to the user's mobility [\[33\]](#page-53-5), [\[34\]](#page-53-6) which is often ignored by researchers [\[35\]](#page-53-7), [\[36\]](#page-53-8), [\[37\]](#page-53-9). Therefore, in order to provide users for seamless access services, efficient mobility management solutions need to be proposed to improve the uses' QoS [\[38\]](#page-53-10). In particular, mobility management has the following advantages:

- <span id="page-1-12"></span><span id="page-1-11"></span><span id="page-1-10"></span><span id="page-1-6"></span><span id="page-1-4"></span>• *High energy efficiency:* The mobility of the devices allows the distance between the user and the device to be adjusted in order to consume less transmission power and improve transmission rate, so that the user can optimally upload the task to the corresponding mobile devices.
- *Low latency:* Shortening the distance between mobile devices and users can also reduce the processing delay of user-generated tasks. Therefore, users can offload tasks with higher transmission rates to mobile devices to reduce the time for the task to reach the mobile device.
- *Low interference:* Another problem that fixed mobile devices may face is that they cannot control the interference faced by the signal in the process of transmission. However, this problem can be solved by using mobile devices, which can reduce interference by moving the location.

The above analysis indicates that task offloading, resource allocation, caching and mobility management issues play a vital role in the CETCN. However, there are a few drawbacks when we adopt traditional methods or technologies to solve these issues in the CETCN. For example, traditional methods require an accurate environment model and optimize the problem in one shot, but accurate environment model is difficult for the complex system to obtain. Fortunately, as a broad frontier subject, DRL and MADRL plays an increasingly important role in academia when solving sequential decisionmaking problems and we summarize the advantages of DRL and MADRL as follows:

- Traditional methods require accurate models of the problem, but accurate models for real-world problems are often difficult to obtain. On the contrary, DRL methods often make little assumptions about the environment, and formulate the problem as an Markov decision process (MDP). Due to the little assumption of the environment, the accurate model is not required for DRL methods.
- The problems of task offloading, resource allocation, and caching based on DRL focus on long-term performance optimization, and in this manner, the system can achieve stable performance compared with traditional methods, which are based on one-shot optimization.
- DRL can effectively responds to high-dimensional and dynamic environment states through iterative interactions with the environment, thus its trained agent can obtain the near-optimal policy intelligently.
- MADRL can provide centralized training and decentralized execution framework for the network vendors to make optimal decisions. Thus, the network vendors only require local observation information to make decisions without exchanging information with other vendors. In this manner, the signaling overhead can be significantly reduced.

In summary, integration of the CETCN with existing DRL approaches lays the foundation for creating a new network paradigm. Since this new paradigm introduces DRL and MADRL in all aspects of next-generation mobile communications, it is called AI-enhanced CETCN in this paper. Table [I](#page-2-0) is the abbreviations used in this survey.

## *A. Existing Surveys and Tutorials on Cloud-Edge-Terminal Collaboration*

In the following, we will investigate the relevant research topics that have been summarized in cloud-edge-terminal collaboration network. Specifically, it includes network architecture, computation offloading and scheduling issue, caching issue, mobility management issue, applications and security issues which related to DRL in the CETCN system. It is worth noting that the term "AI-enabled" mentioned in Table [II](#page-3-0) refers to whether the surveys utilize DRL or MADRL to make decisions for computation offloading and scheduling issue, caching issue and mobility management issue.

TABLE II EXISTING SURVEYS AND TUTORIALS ON CLOUD-EDGE NETWORKS

<span id="page-3-4"></span><span id="page-3-3"></span><span id="page-3-1"></span>Moura and Hutchison [\[41\]](#page-53-13) reviewed the literature on applying theoretical game theory, including classical and evolutionary game theory, to wireless networks, emphasizing the upcoming use cases of MEC. Wang et al. [\[42\]](#page-53-14) provided a systematic survey of the architecture design of the connected vehicles based on edge computing and provided a new computation and communication-assisted architecture based on edge computing. In [\[43\]](#page-53-15), the authors analyzed the application scenarios of edge computing and practical implementation methods of deep learning, namely DL training and reasoning in the custom edge computing architecture, and discussed the challenges and future trends of more general and fine-grained intelligence. Qiu et al. [\[44\]](#page-53-16) summarized the scenarios of edge computing in IIoT, then, they proposed a future architecture of the IIoT from the perspective of edge computing, and finally, they analyzed the technological progress of edge computing in communication, load balancing, shared security and application scenarios. In [\[45\]](#page-53-17), machine learning applications in MEC systems were reviewed, and the latest advances in this field prospected. In [\[46\]](#page-53-18), the authors propose a novelty concept-autonomous IoT (AIoT), which combines the IoT and Autonomous Control System (ACS). Moreover, apart from basic DRL approaches, such as DQN, DDPG, A3C, the authors also introduced the multi-agent DRL and its applications in network layer, application layer and perception layer in ACS. Sarmiento et al. [\[47\]](#page-53-19) investigated and analyzed the characteristics and limitations of existing technologies which provide connectivity between sites in the domain of software-defined networking (SDN). They also introduced Kubernetes and discussed some research directions of SDN applications in the distributed cloud edge infrastructure management field. Siriwardhana et al. [\[48\]](#page-53-20) provided an analysis of current and future mobile augmented reality (MAR) systems from the perspective of cloud, edge, terminal, and hybrid architecture options. The requirements and existing issues of the MAR system were discussed, such as communication, mobility and energy management, service offloading, and privacy protection. In [\[49\]](#page-53-21), the authors first introduced a <span id="page-3-11"></span><span id="page-3-10"></span><span id="page-3-2"></span>unified resource scheduling model and then summarized the current research progress of resource allocation based on limited resources at the edge. The authors in [\[50\]](#page-53-22) provided a brief review of the advanced DRL algorithms including actorcritic architecture based DRL algorithms and multi-agent DRL algorithms. Furthermore, they analyzed the cooperation and confliction of multi-agent system. Finally, they reviewed the literature on applying DRL and MADRL algorithms to address issues in IoT scenarios. In [\[51\]](#page-53-23), the authors reviewed the applications of MARL in emerging networks. On the one hand, they introduced the cooperation and confliction relationships in the future network, and specifically, they classified the MARL into fully cooperative setting, fully competitively setting and mixed setting. On the other hand, the authors utilized the MADRL approaches to address the problems in the emerging network, such as network access and control problem, offloading and resource allocation problem and so on.

<span id="page-3-7"></span><span id="page-3-6"></span><span id="page-3-5"></span>In this survey, we provide a comprehensive review of the convergence of the CETCN and AI. Firstly, edge computing is a supplement rather than a substitute for cloud computing, which cannot be discussed separately and independently. Secondly, AI has gained more and more attention in academia in solving decision-making problems. Thirdly, the further enhancement of the computing power of the intelligent terminal enables users to handle decision-making tasks on the terminal devices directly. Therefore, based on the above three reasons, we believe it is necessary to make a full and comprehensive survey of AI-enabled CETCN. In addition, based on the review of existing literature, we summarize some experiences and lessons of the CETCN and point out the application prospects of AI-enabled CETCN.

## <span id="page-3-8"></span>*B. Our Contributions*

<span id="page-3-9"></span>This paper focuses on a specific type of AI technology, i.e., single-agent DRL based on MDP and partial observable MDP (POMDP) and explains that they are insufficient to model

![](_page_4_Figure_2.jpeg)

<span id="page-4-1"></span>Fig. 1. Reading Map of the Survey.

the sequential decision-making problems in the CETCN when involving multiple vendors, i.e., terminal devices, edge devices and cloud. Furthermore, we present MADRL based on Markov Game (MG) and Dec-POMDP to guide different vendors to make optimal decisions in the CETCN. In the following, we present our major contributions of this survey.

- The main issues faced by the CETCN, including task offloading, resource allocation, caching and mobility management issues, these issues that should be addressed to guarantee the high quality of services, such as minimizing the delay and energy consumption. It is worth noting that this is not only helpful for those who focus on AI, but also for those who are interested in solving the CETCN problems.
- Additionally, we present a brief explanation of AI algorithms which is the mainstream in the current industry and academia to enable future researchers to use this paradigm as an important tool for future studies of the CETCN.
- We comprehensively review the recent works and use cases in the literature that utilize AI to improve the network performance of the CETCN.
- We point out the further development directions for the convergence of deep reinforcement learning and CETCN.

## *C. Paper Organization and Reading Map*

In the following, we give a brief introduction to the structure of this article. The first part of this paper mainly includes Section [I](#page-0-0) which is the introduction, Section [II](#page-4-0) which introduces the related concepts of the CETCN system, such as cloud computing, fog computing and MEC, and Section [III](#page-9-0) introduces the related concepts of reinforcement learning, several state-of-the-art reinforcement learning algorithms and their links with the CETCN. The second part adopts a deduction and summary structure. In Section [IV,](#page-13-0) it first introduces various cloud edge collaboration architectures, and then gives a comprehensive overview of the main issues in the cloud edge collaboration architecture, mainly including task offloading, resource allocation, collaborative caching and mobility management in Section [V,](#page-15-0) Sections [VI](#page-24-0) and [VII.](#page-29-0) Finally, we listed two scenarios of cloud edge collaborative architecture application, namely intelligent transportation system and industrial Internet of Things in Section [VIII.](#page-33-0) The last part lists the challenges and possible development directions of applying AI in the CETCN in Section [XI,](#page-50-0) and finally summarizes the full text in Section [XII.](#page-52-27) The reading map is shown in Fig. [1.](#page-4-1)

## II. RELATED CONCEPTS

<span id="page-4-0"></span>In this section, we introduce and compare several key concepts in the CETCN. The following subsections introduce the main concepts in cloud computing, fog computing and edge computing in Sections [II-A–](#page-4-2)[II-C,](#page-7-0) and then we integrate these concepts in Fig. [2.](#page-5-0) We also discuss their similarities and differences in Section [II-D](#page-8-0) with a Venn diagram in Fig. [3.](#page-8-1) Finally, we introduce the cloud-edge-terminal collaborative computing, which can take full advantages of the above mentioned computing paradigms in Section [II-E.](#page-8-2)

## <span id="page-4-2"></span>*A. Cloud Computing*

<span id="page-4-3"></span>Cloud computing evolved from traditional distributed computing and has become a relatively popular Internet infrastructure service. Network virtualization [\[52\]](#page-53-24), [\[53\]](#page-53-25) is one of the essential supporting technologies of cloud computing which is based on virtual machine (VM). It connects

![](_page_5_Figure_2.jpeg)

<span id="page-5-0"></span>Fig. 2. The main components of cloud-edge-fog/terminal system.

<span id="page-5-3"></span><span id="page-5-1"></span>and combines various resources as a whole at the bottom in a particular way, and provides them for individuals and businesses on demand [\[54\]](#page-53-26), [\[55\]](#page-53-27), [\[56\]](#page-53-28). It is universally acknowledged that the service type of cloud computing can be divided into Infrastructure-as-a-Service (IaaS) [\[57\]](#page-53-29), [\[58\]](#page-53-30), [\[59\]](#page-53-31), Platform-as-a-Service (PaaS) [\[60\]](#page-53-32), [\[61\]](#page-53-33), [\[62\]](#page-53-34), and Softwareas-a-Service (SaaS) [\[63\]](#page-53-35), [\[64\]](#page-53-36), [\[65\]](#page-53-37) according to the different service levels. However, with the emergence of cloud-edgedevice collaborative networks, cloud computing has been unable to meet the requirements of the emerging applications. In the following, we summary the problems and challenges faced by cloud computing.

- <span id="page-5-5"></span><span id="page-5-4"></span>*1) Challenges of Cloud Computing:* Cloud computing has revolutionized the way organizations and individuals access and manage computing resources. However, it also brings considerable challenges [\[66\]](#page-53-38), [\[67\]](#page-53-39). Therefore, we list several key challenges of cloud computing in the following:
  - *Data Availability and Reliability [\[68\]](#page-53-40), [\[69\]](#page-53-41):* Cloud service outages can result in temporary unavailability of services and data. Operators need to carefully consider service-level agreements (SLAs) to ensure adequate uptime and reliability.
  - *Data Transfer and Latency [\[70\]](#page-53-42)*: Uploading and downloading large volumes of data from the cloud servers can be time-consuming and bandwidth-intensive. High latency can impact the performance of real-time applications. Specifically, on the one hand, for real-time

- applications that require immediate response, connecting devices to remote cloud servers can bring intolerable delays. On the other hand, for mobile users in remote areas, relying on cloud services may result in the inability to access certain services.
- <span id="page-5-8"></span><span id="page-5-2"></span>• *Cost Management [\[71\]](#page-53-43), [\[72\]](#page-53-44):* Cloud services typically involve pay-as-you-go pricing models. While this model can offer cost savings for some users, it can also lead to unexpected costs if not carefully monitored and managed. For example, using service level agreements (SLAs) that exceed the requirements can result in premium fees. In addition, complex pricing models can also lead to additional costs.
- <span id="page-5-9"></span>• *Performance and Scalability [\[73\]](#page-53-45):* Although scalability allows programs to accommodate to varying workloads by allocating or deallocating resources, the scalability of the cloud computing can be affected by various factors, including server load, network congestion, the existence of multi-tenant environment and cost.
- <span id="page-5-10"></span><span id="page-5-7"></span><span id="page-5-6"></span>• *Security and Privacy issue [\[74\]](#page-54-0)*: Data security and privacy are major concerns in cloud computing. Storing sensitive data and applications on third-party servers raise questions about data breaches. In addition, due to the large amount of information stored on cloud devices through the network, there are many vulnerabilities that can be exploited by malicious actors.

<span id="page-6-0"></span>Serverless computing, also known as Function-as-a-Service (FaaS), is a popular cloud computing execution model in which the cloud provider can dynamically manage server resources [\[75\]](#page-54-1). In a serverless architecture, although developers write and deploy individual functions or code snippets, and the cloud provider takes care of automatically scaling, managing, and maintaining the underlying infrastructure to execute those functions, serverless computing also faces the following challenges [\[76\]](#page-54-2), [\[77\]](#page-54-3):

- <span id="page-6-1"></span>• *Cold Start Latency:* When a serverless function is invoked after being idle for a period, there can be a delay known as a "cold start". This occurs because the cloud provider needs to allocate and initialize the resources required to execute the function. Cold start latency can impact real-time and highly responsive applications.
- *Resource Limitations:* Serverless platforms impose resource limitations on individual functions, such as maximum execution time and memory allocation. This can be challenging for tasks that require extensive computation or have specific resource requirements.
- *Vendor Lock-In:* Adopting a serverless platform often means relying heavily on the features and APIs provided by the specific cloud provider. This can lead to vendor lock-in, making it difficult to switch to a different provider without significant code modifications.
- *Stateless Execution:* Serverless functions are typically stateless, which means they donot retain data between invocations. Handling state and maintaining session information across function calls can be challenging and may require additional mechanisms, such as external databases or caching solutions.
- *Testing and Deployment:* Testing serverless applications can be more complex due to the event-driven nature and potential interactions between different functions. Additionally, deploying and managing a set of interrelated functions can require careful coordination.
- *Scalability and Performance:* Although serverless platforms offer automatic scaling, they may not handle sudden or extreme spikes in demand as effectively as anticipated, and thus resulting in a decrease in service quality due to insufficient instance resources.
- *2) Challenges of Emerging Applications to the Traditional Cloud Computing:* With the increasing number of applications and their demands for wider bandwidth and lower latency, these emerging applications and demands also bring great challenges to cloud computing which can be summarized as follows:
  - *Efficient distribution of content:* Media content distribution, occupies the vast majority of the network resources. Due to the repeated request of the popular media content, the centralized distribution mode causes a considerable waste of bandwidth. Also, it brings difficulties in supporting larger-scale users and large-scale data distribution services.
  - *Task offloading:* Since smart mobile terminals are limited in computing and storage capabilities, how to exploit the resources of cloud computing or other adjacent devices to assist in completing tasks is becoming

- increasingly important. Task offloading is an excellent way to overcome the resource limitation problem of mobile terminals. However, since the user experience of these applications relies heavily on latency, these applications are not suitable for offloading to remote cloud computing centers for execution.
- *Mobile big data analysis:* A large number of applications of smart mobile devices generate massive data, and the analysis and processing of these data can accurately locate the enterprise market and product applications. Traditional batch or streaming data processing that relies on data centers will bring high bandwidth usage and latency.
- *Intelligent Transportation/Government/Medical applications:* If these intelligent field applications, such as traffic information release, traffic scheduling, accident and emergency handling, are all centrally processed in remote data centers, in that case, it is likely to miss the best processing opportunity due to computing or transmission delays.

It can be seen from the above analysis that centralized cloud computing is challenging to satisfy the emerging applications and services due to the heterogeneity and intelligence of terminal equipment, especially for the IoT and various realtime interactive applications. Therefore, it has become urgent to explore new network computing models to make up for the shortcomings of cloud computing.

## *B. Multi-Access Edge Computing*

<span id="page-6-2"></span>The centralized processing mode of cloud computing has become the obstacle of the evolution of ever-changing ubiquitous terminals [\[78\]](#page-54-4), [\[79\]](#page-54-5), ubiquitous network technologies, and new networked applications and services. Therefore, the industry and academia have begun exploring and researching the other computing models [\[80\]](#page-54-6). Since Cisco proposed fog computing in 2011, the concept of similar computing models in the post-cloud computing era has been proposed successively in different noun forms, such as edge computing [\[81\]](#page-54-7).

<span id="page-6-5"></span><span id="page-6-4"></span><span id="page-6-3"></span>Specifically, the data centers were initially deployed centrally far away from terminals and have been extended to the network edge [\[82\]](#page-54-8). In specific technical implementation, the network edge can be not only edge routers, communication base stations, servers, etc., but also specially deployed servers, small data centers, or even other terminal devices with idle resources. In this manner, the impact of delays caused by longdistance networks and heterogeneous wireless networks can be solved to a certain extent. These new network computing models are a natural continuation of cloud computing from a centralized processing model to a decentralized and small centralized model [\[83\]](#page-54-9).

<span id="page-6-7"></span><span id="page-6-6"></span>The concept of mobile edge computing (MEC) was first proposed by ETSI in September 2014 [\[84\]](#page-54-10). It is a nearend cloud computing model proposed by communication equipment manufacturers, hence its purpose focuses on the support for communication and applications and does not focus on too much technical content.

<span id="page-7-1"></span>Multi-access Edge Computing was evolved and renamed from Mobile Edge Computing in September 2016. MEC aims to overcome the challenges faced by cloud computing in cellular scenarios, especially in emerging 5G/6G application scenarios [\[85\]](#page-54-11), [\[86\]](#page-54-12), [\[87\]](#page-54-13). With the development of access technology, access paths are not limited to mobile cellular networks but also include Wi-Fi networks and wired networks. Therefore, ETSI renamed its concept to multi-access edge computing based on maintaining the MEC abbreviation. In the following, when we mention MEC, we do not distinguish between mobile edge computing and multi-access edge computing unless otherwise stated.

<span id="page-7-5"></span><span id="page-7-3"></span><span id="page-7-2"></span>It is possible to deploy MEC servers at the LTE Macro Base Station (eNB) [\[88\]](#page-54-14), [\[89\]](#page-54-15), 3G Radio Network Controller (RNC) sites [\[90\]](#page-54-16), [\[91\]](#page-54-17), [\[92\]](#page-54-18). Since cellular aggregation sites can be installed both inside enterprises (indoors) [\[93\]](#page-54-19), and in specific public places (outdoors) [\[94\]](#page-54-20) to cover specific scenarios, they can provide local fast services to users directly from base station clusters. The MEC server platform provided by ETSI ISG MEC includes a MEC hosting infrastructure [\[95\]](#page-54-21) and a MEC application platform [\[96\]](#page-54-22), [\[97\]](#page-54-23).

<span id="page-7-7"></span>Of course, the server platform provided by MEC can support and provide a variety of mobile communication value-added services and applications, such as augmented reality content transmission [\[98\]](#page-54-24), video analysis [\[99\]](#page-54-25), content optimization [\[100\]](#page-54-26) and content caching [\[101\]](#page-54-27), etc.

<span id="page-7-10"></span><span id="page-7-8"></span>In recent years, because MEC is proximity to terminal devices, combining the decentralized and decentralized computing models and supporting end collaboration, MEC shows great development opportunities in many application areas [\[102\]](#page-54-28) such as the IoT, 5G, smart grid, virtual/augmented reality, and drones. Compared with cloud computing, MEC can achieve lower latency, save energy and enhance security for users. The advantages are described briefly as follows.

<span id="page-7-13"></span><span id="page-7-12"></span>*Low latency***:** Service delay consists of propagation delay, computation delay, and communication delay, which depends on propagation distance, computing power, and data rate, respectively. First of all, for the MEC network, the information propagation distance is usually tens of meters, generally no more than 1 kilometer. In contrast, cloud computing needs to transmit information from end users to the core network, with a distance ranging from tens of kilometers to thousands of kilometers. In this manner, the propagation delay of the MEC is shorter than cloud computing. Second, the transmission of user information from the terminal device to the cloud center needs to go through the wireless access network, the backhaul network and the Internet which leads to increased delays. Last but not least, the computing resources on cloud servers are richer than those on edge devices. But at the same time, computing resources on the cloud will be shared by more users. These two factors narrow the gap between cloud computing and MEC in computing latency. Experiments demonstrate that the delay caused by cloud computing is in the range 30-100ms which is intolerable for latency sensitive services, such as online games which require processing latency within 1ms [\[103\]](#page-54-29). In contrast, MEC is expected to be a promising technology for implementing delay-sensitive applications due to its shorter transmission distance and simpler protocol.

<span id="page-7-14"></span>*Energy saving***:** Because IoT devices are usually powered by batteries, IoT devices cannot cooperate to complete large and complex tasks [\[104\]](#page-54-30). Powering massive IoT devices is still a critical problem in designing IoT systems, as it is impossible and impractical to replace the batteries of the IoT devices frequently. Therefore, by offloading part of the tasks to MEC servers, the battery life of IoT devices can be prolonged. Some energy saving experiments have been carried out to demonstrate the effectiveness of computing offloading, such as in [\[105\]](#page-54-31), [\[106\]](#page-54-32).

<span id="page-7-16"></span><span id="page-7-15"></span><span id="page-7-4"></span>*Enhanced privacy and security***:** The traditional cloud computing system is consisted of the remote public data centers, which are highly vulnerable to intruders. In addition, since the management permissions of users' data are separated in cloud computing, it will cause the problem of privacy data leakage [\[107\]](#page-54-33) and this issue can be handled by using MEC servers. On the one hand, distributed deployment reduces the centralization of valuable information. On the other hand, MEC servers can be private cloudlets, which will reduce the risk of privacy leakage. For example, the enterprise manages data by themselves through deploying MEC servers, thereby the data security can be ensured [\[108\]](#page-54-34).

## <span id="page-7-17"></span><span id="page-7-9"></span><span id="page-7-6"></span><span id="page-7-0"></span>*C. Fog Computing*

<span id="page-7-19"></span><span id="page-7-18"></span><span id="page-7-11"></span>Fog Computing is introduced by Cisco [\[109\]](#page-54-35) to address the problems faced by cloud computing. It is a novel computing paradigm that can sink the various resources to the network edge. However, unlike MEC which mentioned above, fog is connected to the cloud closely [\[110\]](#page-54-36), in other words, it cannot running in an independent way. With fog computing, the processing of some applications can take place at the network edge and thus can provide low latency services to the users. It also provides densely distributed fog nodes for collecting data generated by terminal devices. These fog nodes include access points and routers which are closer to the users.

<span id="page-7-20"></span>The fog computing system is composed of three parts, including cloud servers, fog servers and IoT/end users [\[111\]](#page-54-37). The fog servers consist of one or more fog regions which can be owned and controlled by different providers. Each fog region is consisted of many fog nodes, including gateways, access points, etc. The IoT/end user is consisted by two entities, one is user devices, the other is IoT devices. It is noteworthy that one of these two entities can be absented in the real-world system.

<span id="page-7-21"></span>Fog computing has several critical characteristics [\[112\]](#page-54-38) that are summarized as follows. (1) Heterogeneity, fog computing nodes can include access points (APs), edge routers, RSUs, and even mobile terminals (such as smart phones). (2) Decentralized management, different from cloud computing, fog computing nodes are deployed distributed at the network edge, and in this manner, the fog nodes can manage its resources in a distributed way. (3) Support for interacting with the cloud. The delay-sensitive applications can be executed at the fog nodes, while computation-intensive applications and delay-tolerant applications can be executed at the cloud servers due to its powerful computing capability.

![](_page_8_Figure_2.jpeg)

<span id="page-8-1"></span>Fig. 3. The relationship between MEC, Fog Computing and Cloud Computing.

## <span id="page-8-0"></span>*D. Discussion the Differences of the Three Computing Modes*

This section focuses on the similarities and differences among cloud computing, fog computing, and edge computing. From a geographical perspective, these three computing modes range from core networks to edge networks. Therefore, it is necessary to point out several differences of these three computing modes.

The first difference is that cloud computing and fog computing are proposed by Google and Cisco, respectively. For example, the OpenFog consortium, it aims to accelerate the deployment of Fog technology by developing core technologies such as open architecture, distributed computing, networking and storage. Only MEC is proposed by the industrial consortium and the initial purpose of MEC focuses on business and interests. It aims to combine information industries such as communication and IT, and further increase IT and cloud computing service capabilities in the Radio Access Network (RAN). The second difference is that MEC operates in a stand-alone mode and fog computing operates in a stand-alone mode or connects to cloud.

The third difference is that the applications targeted by these three computing modes are different. Cloud computing is responsible for big data analysis of long-period data, and can operate in areas such as periodic maintenance and business decision-making. The principle of fog computing is the same as cloud computing, which uploads data to remote centers for analysis, storage and processing. However, fog computing sets up many scattered central nodes, namely the so-called "fog nodes" to process, which can make the calculation process faster and more efficient to obtain the calculation results. Edge computing focuses on the analysis of real-time and short-term data to better support the timely processing and execution

of local businesses. Fig. [3](#page-8-1) provides a summary of MEC, fog computing and cloud computing.

## <span id="page-8-2"></span>*E. Cloud-Edge-Terminal Collaborative Computing*

<span id="page-8-3"></span>With the further enhancement of the computation and caching capabilities of terminal devices, the use of terminal devices for task offloading, resource allocation, cooperative caching, and mobility management decisions can further reduce energy consumption and transmission delay in the CETCN. In fact, there is currently no internationally recognized definition for CETCN. In order to solve this problem, the industrial and academic communities have made many efforts [\[113\]](#page-54-39), [\[114\]](#page-54-40), [\[115\]](#page-54-41), [\[116\]](#page-54-42). We briefly depict the CETCN in Fig. [2](#page-5-0) and give a definition of CETCN in the following. *In this paper, we define the CETCN as a distributed computing architecture that consists of three layer, including the terminal layer, the edge layer and the cloud layer. This architecture is designed to optimize the computing, storage, and communication resources of terminal devices, edge devices and cloud devices to cater to the demands of various emerging applications and services.* Furthermore, In a broader sense, the collaborative computing can be the terminal-edge collaboration, edge-edge collaboration, edgecloud collaboration, and terminal-edge-cloud collaboration. In this collaborative system, the terminal layer includes a large amount of terminal devices, such as sensors, vehicles, AR/VR devices, intelligent wearable devices with very limited computing capability, and wireless access points used for communication between terminal layer and edge layer. The edge layer consists of not only edge devices, such as edge servers used for processing and storing data, but also consists of base stations, WiFi and LoRa used for managing network traffic and acting as intermediaries between the terminal layer and the cloud layer. These edge devices are widely deployed at urban centers, factory area and densely populated areas. The cloud layer is composed of various cloud servers, the number of deployed cloud servers depends on population density, business landscape, data-intensive applications and so on. Moreover, the authors in [\[113\]](#page-54-39), [\[114\]](#page-54-40), [\[115\]](#page-54-41), [\[116\]](#page-54-42) proposed a CETCN framework, which is different from the one proposed in this work. The main difference is that we have added some emerging scenarios with potential applications for CETCN at the terminal layer, such as industrial IoT and smart transportation. At the edge layer, we have also added other radio access methods such as WiFi and LoRa. In the cloud layer, we have provided a detailed description of the components of the cloud server which does not mentioned in [\[113\]](#page-54-39), [\[114\]](#page-54-40), [\[115\]](#page-54-41), [\[116\]](#page-54-42).

Moreover, Fig. [2](#page-5-0) also shows a comprehensive picture of the data life cycle management in future wireless networks [\[117\]](#page-54-43), including data generation and analysing at the terminal layer, cross layer transmission of data and data processing at edge/cloud layer. By integrating AI into the data life cycle of wireless networks, it becomes possible to enhance efficiency, improve performance, and address challenges associated with massive data volumes and complex network operations. Therefore, in the following, we summary several key aspects of wireless AI for the data life cycle management.

- *Data Collection and Preprocessing:* AI techniques, such as machine learning and deep learning algorithms, can be used to preprocess the data and clean the redundant data collected from wireless network devices and sensors [\[118\]](#page-54-44). This process can ensure that the data used for analysis and decision-making is accurate and reliable [\[119\]](#page-54-45).
- <span id="page-9-4"></span><span id="page-9-3"></span><span id="page-9-2"></span>• *Data Transmission and Communication:* AI can optimize the wireless communication process by managing data traffic, allocating resources, and predicting network congestion [\[120\]](#page-55-0), [\[121\]](#page-55-1). For example, AI-driven adaptive modulation and coding schemes can dynamically adjust transmission parameters to improve data transfer rates based on the channel conditions [\[122\]](#page-55-2).
- <span id="page-9-7"></span>• *Data Processing and Analytics:* AI algorithms can perform real-time data analysis [\[123\]](#page-55-3), pattern recognition [\[124\]](#page-55-4), and abnormal detection [\[125\]](#page-55-5). This enables intelligent decision-making, predictive maintenance, and data-driven optimizations for various applications, such as network management and IoT applications.
- *Data Storage and Management:* AI can be employed to optimize data storage and management strategies. For instance, AI-driven data de-duplication and compression techniques can reduce storage requirements and enhance data retrieval speed [\[126\]](#page-55-6).
- <span id="page-9-9"></span>• *Data Utilization and Application:* AI can enhance the utilization of data collected from wireless networks by enabling advanced applications like predictive maintenance [\[127\]](#page-55-7), intelligent traffic management [\[128\]](#page-55-8), [\[129\]](#page-55-9), and personalized services.
- <span id="page-9-13"></span><span id="page-9-10"></span>• *Network Resource Management:* AI-based techniques can be used for dynamic spectrum allocation [\[130\]](#page-55-10), interference mitigation [\[131\]](#page-55-11), [\[132\]](#page-55-12), and energy-efficient management of network resources [\[133\]](#page-55-13).

• *Security and Privacy:* AI can be employed to detect and mitigate security threats in wireless networks. Additionally, AI can help enforce privacy policies and ensure compliance with data protection regulations [\[134\]](#page-55-14).

However, in the CETCN system, there are still several issues need to be addressed. We summarize the issues as follows:

- <span id="page-9-15"></span>• *Heterogenous capacity of edge servers and terminal devices:* The further development of wireless network technology makes the emerging network services generate extra energy consumption and delay problems. At the same time, due to the size limitations of edge and terminal devices and the differences in computing and caching capabilities among different devices, it is a challenging problem to reasonably utilize limited edge resources to provide differentiated services to users.
- <span id="page-9-1"></span>• *Large-dimensional and multi-objective problems:* Due to the coupling of various resources, the optimization problem often have multiple optimization objectives with different constraints. At the same time, with the rapid growth of terminal devices, the dimension of the optimization problem will be further expanded, which resulting in NP-hard joint optimization problems.
- *Mixed-integer programming:* Mixed integer programming often appears in CETCN optimization problems. As an example, for the typical problem of task offloading in the CETCN, the discrete caching decision variable and the continuous task offloading variable should be jointly considered.

<span id="page-9-16"></span>From the above analysis, it can be seen that due to the heterogeneity and limitations of edge devices, including edge servers and terminal devices, as well as the dynamic nature of resource requirements of various applications, it is still challenging to achieve efficient resource allocation while performing tasks [\[135\]](#page-55-15). Most of the existing algorithms can achieve efficient task offloading, resource allocation, collaboratice caching and mobility management in specific scenarios, however, they lack a unified framework to study these decision-making problems in practical scenarios, which consists of multiple criteria, such as heterogeneity, scalability, and mobility [\[136\]](#page-55-16).

<span id="page-9-19"></span><span id="page-9-18"></span><span id="page-9-17"></span><span id="page-9-8"></span><span id="page-9-6"></span><span id="page-9-5"></span>In principle, algorithms based on reinforcement learning obtain optimal solutions on the basis of long-term optimization by continuously learning the changes of the dynamic environment [\[137\]](#page-55-17), [\[138\]](#page-55-18). For example, a RL-based task offloading algorithm was developed to reduce the average transmission delay of tasks [\[139\]](#page-55-19). In recent years, the combination of deep learning and reinforcement learning which called DRL has further enhanced the decision-making ability when facing complex and uncertain environments [\[140\]](#page-55-20), [\[141\]](#page-55-21). In this regards, We summarize some popular AI algorithms to set the stage for our selected issues in CETCN.

## <span id="page-9-20"></span><span id="page-9-11"></span><span id="page-9-0"></span>III. AN INTRODUCTION TO REINFORCEMENT LEARNING AND ITS STATE-OF-THE-ART

<span id="page-9-14"></span><span id="page-9-12"></span>The aim of this section is to introduce the mathematical background of SARL and MARL in Sections [III-A](#page-10-0) and [III-B,](#page-10-1) and briefly reveal its connection with the CETCN through several examples. We also introduce several DRL algorithms used in this survey in Section III-C.

#### <span id="page-10-0"></span>A. Single-Agent Reinforcement Learning

In this subsection, we summarize the MDP and POMDP which is useful in modelling the RL problems. For more details, the interested readers can refer to the following literature [142], [143], [144].

<span id="page-10-2"></span>1) Markov Decision Process: We introduce the MDP through reinforcement learning model. Reinforcement learning maximizes its reward by interacting with the environment and taking corresponding actions. RL is generally regarded as an MDP and defined by a tuple  $\langle \mathcal{S}, \mathcal{A}, R, T, \gamma \rangle$ . At each episode, the agent receives a state  $s \in \mathcal{S}$ , selects an action  $a \in \mathcal{A}$  and gets a reward  $r \in R$  according to the policy, and then transits to the next state [145]. We often use a discount factor  $\gamma$  to express the impact of future rewards on the present.

In the following, we describe the key components of reinforcement learning in a more mathematical perspective.

- <span id="page-10-3"></span>• state( $s_t$ ): The state is generally discrete in reinforcement learning, and  $s_t$  generally represents the state of the agent at time step t.
- $action(a_t)$ : This is the output of the agent, when an agent observes the environment state, it will take an action  $a_t$  as the feedback of  $s_t$ .
- policy( $\pi$ ): The policy  $\pi$  represents the probability that the agent chooses an action a based on the state s and  $\pi(a|s) = P(a_t = a|s_t = s)$
- state transition probability  $(P^a_{ss'})$ : state transition probability depicts the probability when an agent takes action a and transits to the next state s'.
- reward( $r_t$ ): When the agent takes an action, the environment will give the agent a reward. In general, the actions taken by the agent will have an impact on subsequent rewards. Therefore, to measure this effect, we use a discount factor  $\gamma$  to accumulate the rewards that the agent subsequently receives and can be represented as  $R_t = \sum_{t=1}^{\infty} \gamma^t r_t$ .
- value function: The purpose of RL is to learn a policy that maximizes the expected return. We define the state-value function as

$$V^{\pi}(s) = \mathbf{E}_{\pi}[R_t|s_t = s]$$

we can also define a state-action value function and can be represented as

$$Q^{\pi}(s, a) = \mathbf{E}_{\pi}[R_t | s_t = s, a_t = a]$$

Example: Several CETCN problems can be formulated as an MDP. For example, the authors in [146] model the task offloading and resource allocation problem as an MDP. The agent is the set of *N* mobile users. The state space is consisted of the computing resource. The action space is defined as the new task offloading decision and resource allocation decision. For each time step, the agent chooses an action and receives a reward, then transfers to the next state. Due to the continuity of the state and action space, the RL algorithm which called DDPG can be adopted to solve the problem under continuous action and state space.

<span id="page-10-5"></span>2) Partial Observable Markov Decision Process: MDP usually assumes that the agent can obtain all the information in the environment. However, this assumption is difficult to realize in most practical systems. For example, the edge server can only obtain the tasks which generated from its connected terminal users. Thus, the agent only has part information of the whole system to make decisions. Hence, the problem describes above can be modelled as the POMDP [147], which extends the MDP to the partial observable environment. Specifically, a POMDP is denoted by a 7-tuple  $\langle S, A, R, T, \gamma, \mathcal{O}, Z \rangle$ , where S, A, R, T and  $\gamma$  denotes the state, action, reward, state transition probability and discount factor, respectively, which is similar to the MDP, the observation is represented by  $\mathcal{O}$ , and  $Z: \mathcal{S} \times \mathcal{A} \times \mathcal{O} \rightarrow [0,1]$  denotes the transition probability based on a state  $s \in \mathcal{S}$ , an action  $a \in \mathcal{A}$  and an observation  $o \in \mathcal{O}$ . In other words, the agent will receive a new observation when the agent chooses action a and the environment transmits from s to the next state s'.

<span id="page-10-6"></span>Example: Several problems in the wireless communication system usually can be modelled as a POMDP. For example, the terminal equipments cannot obtain all the channel state information of the whole system, and can only obtain the estimation of the channel state information through interaction with the environment. Hence, in [148], the authors propose a dynamic task offloading problem in fog computing system based on POMDP. The agent is the set of IoT devices and has a sequence of computation tasks that need to be processed. Due to the limited computing capacity of IoT devices, the IoT devices need to decide whether tasks require to be processed at the local or offloaded to the fog servers to process through wireless link. If the task is decided to offload to the fog server to execute, the IoT devices need to consider which sub-channel to connect based on the estimated channel state information. Since the agent can not obtain the full information about the available sub-channels, this task offloading problem is considered as a POMDP. A finite state Markov chain is adopted due to the sub-channels fading is block fading. Based on the model, the IoT devices can estimate the channel state information (CSI) about the sub-channels and then choose a subset to connect for transmission. Hence, the state space is consisted of quantized CSI and the queue length, and the observation space of the agents is queue length. Given the observation of the agent, the agents need to minimize the task processing energy consumption under the maximum tolerable delay.

## <span id="page-10-4"></span><span id="page-10-1"></span>B. Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL)

Multi-agent reinforcement learning solves the sequence decision problem involving the interaction of multiple players. Therefore, the dynamic characteristics of the system are mainly determined by the joint actions of players. In other words, the reward of an agent is no longer a function of its own actions, but depends on the joint actions of all players involved in the interaction. Therefore, in order to maximize long-term benefits, one player needs to consider the actions of other players when taking actions. In the following, we give a brief introduction of MARL from a mathematical view

![](_page_11_Picture_2.jpeg)

Fig. 4. Multi agents acting in the environment.

<span id="page-11-0"></span>and give several example to illustrate the benefits of using MARL.

1) Markov Game: The communication network such as the CETCN involves multiple network entities such as edge nodes or mobile users. These entities not only interact with the environment, but also with other entities. Therefore, considering the interaction between entities, it is necessary to develop effective MARL algorithms for future Internet problems.

<span id="page-11-1"></span>The multi-agent MDP can be defined by Markov Game [149] or Stochastic Game [150], as can be seen in Fig. 4, joint action is the result of each agent's independent choice of action. As shown in Fig. 4, the MG is defined by 5-tuple  $(\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{N}, \{\mathcal{A}_i\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}}, \mathcal{T}, \{R_i\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}}, \gamma)$ , where  $\mathcal{S}$  is the state space, also called joint observation.  $\mathcal{N} = \{1, 2, \dots, N\}$  denotes the set of the agents,  $\mathcal{A} = A_1 \times A_2 \times \cdots \times A_{\mathcal{N}}$  is the joint action space. In the area of the CETCN, the states of each agent include the various indicators of devices. The action state can be the allocated bandwidth, the allocated computing cycles and the caching status. The reward functions are usually related to the optimization objectives, such as minimizing the delay or maximizing the transmission rate.

At time step t, each agent chooses an action  $a_t^i$  based on the current system state  $s_t$ . Then, the state  $s_t$  transmits to the next state  $s_{t+1}$ , and receives the reward  $R_i(s_t, a_t, s_{t+1})$ . The aim of each agent is to find a policy  $\pi_i^*: S \to A_i$  to maximize its own reward. Specifically, in multi-agent systems, the value function  $V^i: S \to \mathbb{R}$  of agent i is represented by all the agents' policies,

$$V_{\pi^{i},\pi^{-i}}^{i}(s) := \mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T} \gamma^{t} R_{i}(s_{t}, a_{t}, s_{t+1})_{a_{t}^{i} \sim \pi^{i}(\cdot | s_{t}), s_{0} = s}\right]$$

where -i represents the agents that expect agent i. The above equation indicates the common solution of the MG is to find an optimal solution for each agent that no agent will receive a better solution under the situation that other agents do not change their solutions, and this statement is true for all agents. We call this optimal solution as Nash equilibrium (NE) [151], such that

<span id="page-11-3"></span>
$$V_{\pi^{i,*}\pi^{-i,*}}^{i}(s) \ge V_{\pi^{i}\pi^{-i,*}}^{i}(s).$$

The NE represents that the optimal policy  $\pi^{i,*}$  is the best response of  $\pi^{-i,*}$  for all the agents. Generally speaking, it

<span id="page-11-4"></span>always exists in finite-space infinite discounted MGs [152] and NE is often more than one.

Example: In particular, the relationship among agents can be divided into cooperative and competition when involves multi-agent environment. Both of these two situations are common in the CETCN. For example, the terminal devices can cooperative with each other to achieve a higher transmission rate. However, when it comes to task offloading issues, the terminal devices may compete with each other to obtain limited edge resources to reduce the task processing delay as much as possible. The MGs can handle the following MARL settings: full cooperative setting, full competitive setting and mixed setting. In cooperative setting, in order to avoid low efficiency (avoid lazy agents), the authors in [153] propose team-average reward, and the goal is to maximize the longterm average reward. When in the full competitive setting, the MGs can be seen as zero-sum MGs. In the mixed setting, the MG consists of the cooperative and competitive agents [154]. The most popular MARL algorithm which called MADDPG [155], have been developed for mixed setting to calculate the Nash equilibrium solution of the MGs.

<span id="page-11-8"></span><span id="page-11-7"></span><span id="page-11-6"></span><span id="page-11-5"></span><span id="page-11-2"></span>2) Dec-POMDP: In particular, the agents cannot receive the whole system information timely in the CETCN and the uncertainty of other agents and the environment has a great influence on learning an optimal solution. In this case, the Partial Observable Markov Game (POMG) under cooperative setting can be modelled as Dec-POMDP [156], [157] and it is a directly extension of POMDP from singleagent to multi-agent. The Dec-POMDP is defined by 7-tuple  $(\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{N}, \{\mathcal{A}^i\}_{i \in \mathcal{N}}, P, R, \gamma, (O_i)_{i \in \mathcal{N}}, Z)$ , the first six are the same as the previous definitions of MG,  $O_i$  is the observation space of agent i. In the Dec-POMDP, at each time step, each agent i makes its own decision based on its own observation  $o^i$  instead of system state s. Then, the action  $a^i$  of each agent forms an action set  $A_i = \{a^i | i \in \mathcal{N}\}$  and executes in the environment. The objective of each agent is to learn a policy  $\pi_i: O_i \mapsto A_i$  to maximize its cooperative long-term reward.

<span id="page-11-10"></span><span id="page-11-9"></span>Example: Task offloading [158] and cooperative caching [159] among multiple base stations can be modelled as Dec-POMDP. For example, in [158], the author considers the problem of multi-channel access and task offloading in an industry 4.0 scenario based on MEC. In the proposed scenario, the edge device layer includes N machine type agents (MTAs), such as smart phones, robots, among which the MTAs can better utilize the spectrum resources, save bandwidth and avoid core network congestion through D2D communication. Each MTA can upload and download historical information, including observations and actions from the MTA administrator by D2D communication. Each MTA represents an agent, at each time step, each agent selects an action to minimize the total calculation delay of all the offloaded tasks. The actions of each agent include the state of each task and the selected channel. If the agent i decides to execute the task at the MEC sever, it needs to select the free channel to transmit. The environment is partial observable since each agent can only obtain its own selected channel. Thus, the agent's observation space includes a set of history information about channel selection. Based on the agent's

![](_page_12_Figure_2.jpeg)

<span id="page-12-1"></span>Fig. 5. Learning framework of MADDPG and COMA.

observation, each agent selects an action and receives a reward quantified by the total delay.

## <span id="page-12-0"></span>C. A Brief Introduction of DRL Algorithm Used in This Survey

- <span id="page-12-2"></span>• DQN: DQN solves the problem of unstable convergence [160] of approximate learning state-action value functions by combining Q-learning and deep learning. The first key technique is called **replay buffer**. At each time step *t*, DQN first stores the experience gained by the agent in the replay buffer, and then uniformly samples mini-batches of samples from this buffer for Q-learning updates. The second is called the **target network**, it performs as an independent network to replace the required Q network to generate the Q-learning objective, therefore, further improving the stability of the training process.
- DDQN: Although the DQN algorithm performs well in many games, it has been found that DQN algorithm have the problem of overestimating the Q value. In order to solve the problem, DDQN was proposed by Google DeepMind in 2016 [161]. The core of Double DQN is to try to avoid the overestimation problem by separating the selection action from the evaluation action.
- <span id="page-12-4"></span><span id="page-12-3"></span>• Actor-Critic: This algorithm simultaneously learns the agent's policy function and state value function [162], [163], in addition, the algorithm also applies bootstrapping to estimate the Q-value function. Actor-Critic algorithm through minimizing the square of temporal difference error to learn the critic function.
- <span id="page-12-5"></span>• DDPG: DDPG can be seen as an extension of DQN in continuous action space and can also be seen as a combination of Deterministic Policy Gradient and deep neural network [164]. The DDPG algorithm includes the Q-value function and the policy function at the same time. The Q-value function is updated by the TD method. The policy function is updated by the policy gradient method.
- Multi-agent Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (MADDPG) [155]: It is an extension of DDPG algorithm to multi-agent scenario. As shown in Fig. 5(a), the critic of each agent can evaluate the impact of the joint strategy on the expected reward through obtain other agents' actions. MADDPG can be utilized to solve various issues in the CETCN. For example, MADDPG can be adopted

- to learn the optimal task offloading strategy for each network entity, and in this manner, the computation complexity can also be reduced.
- <span id="page-12-6"></span>• Counterfactual Multi-Agent Policy Gradients (COMA) [165]: As shown in Fig. 5(b) COMA has a local actor to make actions which is similar to MADDPG. However, unlike the MADDPG, the COMA uses the centralized critic to estimates the action-value function. In a multi-agent environment, there exists a credit allocation problem, that is, the multi-agent algorithm uses the status and behavior of all agents to estimate the expected reward, so the agent may not be able to calculate the contribution of its behavior to the total reward. The COMA address the issue through using the counterfactual baseline method, which represented as follows,

$$A^{i}(s, \mathbf{a}) = Q(s, \mathbf{a}) - \sum_{a'^{i}} \pi^{i} \left( a'^{i} | \tau^{i} \right) Q\left(s, (\mathbf{a_{-i}}, a'^{i})\right),$$

where  $Q(s, \mathbf{a})$  is the Q-value of the current joint action  $\mathbf{a}$ , the second term  $\sum_{a'^i} \pi^i(a'^i|\tau^i) Q(s, (\mathbf{a_{-i}}, a'^i))$  is the counterfactual baseline. In addition, COMA uses a single forward pass to compute the counterfactual advantage function for each agent in actor and critic network. Due to COMA uses only one centralized critic, it is only can be used in cooperative scenarios. Moreover, it is impossible to applying COMA in large scale system due to it need to know the global state to calculate the advantage function.

<span id="page-12-7"></span>• Mean-Field Q-Learning (MF-Q): When the joint action space and the number of the agents grows exponentially, MADDPG and COMA may be intractable. To address this issue, mean-field game (MFG) has been proposed to address it [166], [167]. Recently, by combining the MFGs and RL, we can solve MFGs at a very large scale both in terms of population size and environment complexity. Therefore, MF-Q [168] has been proposed to address the above issues. The Q-function is defined as follows

<span id="page-12-8"></span>
$$Q_i(s, \mathbf{a}) = \frac{1}{N_i} \sum_{k \in \mathcal{N}_i} Q_i(s, a_i, a_k) \approx Q_i(s, a_i, \hat{a}_i)$$

where  $\mathcal{N}_i$  is the set of neighboring agents of agent i,  $N_i = |\mathcal{N}(i)|$ . The average value of  $Q_i(s, a_i, a_k)$  can be approximate by  $Q_i(s, a_i, \hat{a}_i)$ . In this manner, the effect of neighboring agents can be converted to the mean-field value  $\hat{a}_i$  and the representation agent can interact with the mean-field term instead of the whole population.

<span id="page-12-10"></span><span id="page-12-9"></span>• Mean-Field Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (MFDDPG): In [169], the authors showed the connection with MDP and they explained how RL can be used to learn MFG solutions in a model-free way. Hence, the last algorithm we discuss is MFDDPG [170] which combine the MFG and DDPG algorithm. In the MFDDPG framework, the MFG can deal with continuous spaces. Similar to DDPG, the key of the algorithm consists in updating the critic network by minimizing an empirical square error and the actor network by making one step of gradient descent.
