Fig. 6. The cloud-edge-terminal cooperation network architecture.

## <span id="page-13-3"></span><span id="page-13-0"></span>IV. THE ARCHITECTURE OF CLOUD-EDGE-TERMINAL COLLABORATIVE NETWORK

This section proposes the role of each component in the CETCN and introduces several potential network architectures of the CETCN in Section [IV-A.](#page-13-1) The strict requirements of current and emerging applications and the improvement of computing power of edge devices jointly realize these architecture options, including cloud-edge based architecture and cloud-edge-terminal based architecture. This section also summarizes the work under each architecture in Sections [IV-B](#page-13-2) and [IV-C.](#page-14-0) Finally, we briefly introduce the connotation of cloud-edge-terminal intelligent collaboration, and explain its characteristics and advantages in emerging applications in Section [IV-D.](#page-15-1)

## <span id="page-13-1"></span>*A. Main Components of Cloud-Edge-Terminal Collaborative Architecture*

There are various cloud-edge-terminal architecture for different applications. As shown in Fig. [6,](#page-13-3) we depict a simplified cloud-edge-terminal collaborative network architecture, which consists of cloud layer, edge layer and terminal layer. The terminal devices are deployed in the terminal layer to collect data for pre-processing and then transmit to the edge layer to computing and processing. Finally, some tasks that edge layer is not able to process will be sent to the cloud layer for execution. The specific roles of each layer are introduced as follows:

• Terminal layer: The first layer is terminal layer, which including sensors and intelligent devices to collect data from the physical environment or generate tasks from users. In some specific cloud edge collaboration systems,

- such as the Internet of Things, terminal devices can also be various sensors, such as infrared sensors, environmental sensors, etc.
- Edge layer: Then, the collected data or the task will be sent to edge layer through communication links. If the computing resources installed in the edge nodes are sufficient to complete the task, then the task will be processed at the edge layer. Due to the distance between terminal layer and edge layer is short and the power for terminal devices are usually supplied by batteries, the communication methods between terminal layer and edge layer should be short distance and low power. Certainly, wireless local area network (WLAN) can provide a stable communication link between terminal layer and edge layer [\[171\]](#page-56-3). However, considering the requirements mentioned above, many other communication methods are more suitable for such transmission, such as ZigBee, Wi-Fi, and Bluetooth [\[172\]](#page-56-4), [\[173\]](#page-56-5).
- <span id="page-13-5"></span><span id="page-13-4"></span>• Cloud layer: Finally, the tasks that the edge layer can not able to process will be sent to cloud layer. The communication distance between edge layer and cloud layer is long and above local transmission methods are not suitable for such transmission. After receiving the tasks from edge layer, the cloud layer will process the task and transmit the results to the terminals through edge layer.

## <span id="page-13-2"></span>*B. Cloud-Edge Collaborative Architecture*

The cloud layer mainly comprises cloud computing architecture, such as computing units, storage units, and data centers. The cloud layer and the edge layer are connected through the core network. Although the edge layer can process tasks from the terminal layer, Those tasks that need in-depth processing still need to be uploaded to the cloud center for processing.

The edge layer is the core layer of the cloud-edge-terminal collaboration architecture. From a hardware perspective, one the one hand, the edge layer contains various network and computing devices, such as edge servers, roadside units, gateways, and edge controllers. On the other hand, the edge layer provides wireless access to a wide variety of smart devices through wireless channels, fiber, and LTE. From a software point of view, the edge layer can manage and orchestrate services. The edge layer can collect and process data from the terminal layer and perform data analysis and real-time control. The edge layer can use its storage capacity to store related data and services for terminal devices. In addition, the cloud layer and the edge layer are complementary to each other and the task of the edge layer can be offloaded to the cloud layer for processing when the resources in the edge layer is not sufficient.

<span id="page-14-3"></span><span id="page-14-1"></span>In [\[174\]](#page-56-6), the authors propose an infrared intelligent service platform which can support cloud-edge cooperation and provide intelligent infrared services. The actual case study showed that the framework simplifies the development of intelligent services and ensures the security of industrial data. In [\[175\]](#page-56-7), to solve the problem of infrastructure switching when the IoT system runs multiple applications for different tasks, this paper proposes a cloud edge cooperation framework based on attention enhancement and verifies the effectiveness of the framework through several most advanced convolutional neural networks. In [\[176\]](#page-56-8), this paper proposes a HIF detection method based on migration learning under the cloud-edge cooperation framework of the IoT system. The framework can integrate all the features in different networks to form a basic cloud convolution neural network model for HIF detection and analyze the performance of the framework through seven different distribution networks. In [\[177\]](#page-56-9), the authors introduce a new edge cloud collaboration architecture to solve resource allocation and decision-making problems and introduces the implementation method of equipment defect detection based on AI to show the effectiveness of the cloud edge collaboration architecture.

<span id="page-14-9"></span><span id="page-14-7"></span><span id="page-14-5"></span>Qing [\[178\]](#page-56-10) propose a cloud-edge collaborative power system that integrates big data and artificial intelligence platforms to realize global virtualization and global perception in the 5G scenario. At the same time, the platform has a certain reference value for cloud-edge collaborative management and control upgrading of a power system. In [\[179\]](#page-56-11), the authors propose a collaborative cloud edge service cognitive framework to deploy the deep learning models, ensuring the reasoning quality and improving the operational efficiency and service reliability. In [\[180\]](#page-56-12), the authors propose an intelligent distributed edge computing system architecture which supports the training and reasoning of deep learning models across heterogeneous resource-constrained IoT devices. In [\[181\]](#page-56-13), to solve the privacy problems faced by many applications, this paper proposes an edge cooperation framework and verifies the effectiveness of the framework through a case study in a real scene. In [\[182\]](#page-56-14), Xu et al. combine deep reinforcement learning and cloud edge collaborative caching to build an intelligent edge caching framework to reduce content redundancy and transmission delay.

<span id="page-14-10"></span>In [\[183\]](#page-56-15), the authors propose a edge-cloud collaborative computing architecture for smart grid which consists four layers. The four layers including perceptual layer, network layer 1, application layer (CC node) and network layer 2. Then, they introduce the applications of smart grid, such as energy generation and transportation. Finally, they point out the future direction of cloud-edge architecture when applying to the smart grid. The authors in [\[184\]](#page-56-16) develop a big data analysis architecture for edgeCcloud computing. In the proposed architecture, an intelligence module is introduced to the edge layer to process the task efficiently. Finally, the experiment results show the efficiency of the proposed architecture.

## <span id="page-14-11"></span><span id="page-14-0"></span>*C. Cloud-Edge-Terminal Collaborative Architecture*

<span id="page-14-2"></span>The terminal layer consists of various terminal devices, such as mobile phones, different automobiles in intelligent transportation systems, and various applications in industrial IoT. These devices all have specific computing and storage capabilities and can continuously generate and collect large amounts of data. Based on the user's service quality requirements, these data can be processed at the terminal layer or offloaded to the edge layer and cloud layer for processing. For example, some applications which require powerful computing ability need to rely on the mighty computing power of the cloud layer. In contrast, low-latency services, such as unmanned driving, may need to process the task nearby and return the processing result to the vehicle in time to avoid traffic accidents.

<span id="page-14-17"></span><span id="page-14-16"></span><span id="page-14-15"></span><span id="page-14-14"></span><span id="page-14-13"></span><span id="page-14-12"></span><span id="page-14-8"></span><span id="page-14-6"></span><span id="page-14-4"></span>To solve the problem of the insufficient computing power of edge devices and the mismatch between edge platform and AI algorithm, Zhang et al. [\[185\]](#page-56-17) construct a lightweight software platform to enable edge intelligent devices to process data and make data sharing between edge devices possible. In [\[186\]](#page-56-18), authors analyze the problem of how to build multiple mobile devices into a group to run artificial intelligence algorithms together. Through this framework, devices can reduce the burden of performing resource-intensive tasks. Nguyen et al. [\[187\]](#page-56-19) propose a task offloading framework in which the parked vehicles are used as the expansion of infrastructure. In the peak period of the network, the edge server is deployed as the primary node, and the parked vehicles are used as subnodes for task offloading. The simulation results show that the framework can effectively and flexibly process tasks. In [\[188\]](#page-56-20), a UAV-driven mobile edge computing framework is proposed. Under this framework, UAVs can help other UAVs perform computing tasks to solve the lack of computing services in remote areas. On this basis, DRL is used to solve the problems of task offloading and resource allocation, which we will discuss in later sections. The authors in [\[189\]](#page-56-21) propose a cache-assisted collaborative intelligence framework between edge devices, which completes the storage and computing tasks of the deep neural network model through the cooperation between edge devices. Due to the limited computing capacity of terminal devices, the authors in [\[190\]](#page-56-22) propose a terminal-edge-cloud collaborative task offloading scheme to achieve the tradeoff between computation and communication capacity. The authors in [\[191\]](#page-56-23) propose a terminal-edge-cloud collaborative architecture to make task offloading decisions for IIoT scenario. Then, they propose a novel task offloading method to obtain the balance between efficiency and delay.

## <span id="page-15-1"></span>*D. Cloud-Edge-Terminal Intelligent Collaboration*

Cloud-edge-terminal intelligent collaboration is not a simple combination of cloud computing, edge computing, and artificial intelligence technologies. Its core value lies in the realization of cloud-edge-terminal endogenous intelligence. At present, there is no internationally recognized definition of cloud-edge-terminal intelligent collaboration. To solve this problem, industry and academia have given different definitions [\[192\]](#page-56-24), [\[193\]](#page-56-25), [\[194\]](#page-56-26). *In this paper, we define cloudedge-terminal intelligent collaboration as the realization of low-cost through and high-efficient collaborative optimization among devices, edges, clouds, and networks.*

<span id="page-15-4"></span>Obviously, in a broad sense, cloud-edge-terminal collaborative intelligence includes different combinations of AI services based on terminals, edge, and cloud, such as terminal-edgeterminal collaboration, edge-cloud collaboration, edge-to-edge collaboration, and terminal-edge-cloud collaboration, as well as cloud intelligence (data are on the cloud, all intelligent analysis and decision-making are completed in the cloud) and terminal intelligence (data acquisition, processing, and intelligent analysis are completed on the terminal device).

It can be seen that cloud-edge-terminal collaborative intelligence in a broad sense is a natural extension of cloud intelligence and terminal intelligence. In some particular scenarios, the optimal collaborative method of cloud-edgeterminal collaborative intelligence may be cloud intelligence, due to the limited resources of terminal and edge device. However, edge-cloud-terminal intelligent collaboration generally finds the optimal collaborative method among all possible collaborative methods to obtain a better collaborative method than pure cloud intelligence and terminal intelligence.

The features and advantages of cloud-edge-terminal intelligent collaboration can be summarized at least from the following four aspects:

- *Lower bandwidth cost*. According to the prediction of the Internet Data Center, by 2025, the total amount of data in the world will reach 180 ZB [\[195\]](#page-56-27), and the current network bandwidth is hard to meet the demand for such data transmission, which will become the bottleneck of cloud computing. The most significant advantage of cloud-edge-terminal intelligent collaboration is that data analysis is processed at the edge, avoiding the need for large amounts of data to be uploaded to the cloud and greatly saving bandwidth costs.
- *Higher level of intelligence*. Cloud-edge-terminal collaborative intelligence can efficiently realize various AI services and applications through the collaboration between devices, edges, and clouds. Deep reinforcement learning is the core support for realizing

- cloud-edge-terminal collaborative intelligence. These AI algorithms are also essential for quickly analyzing large amounts of data. In addition, the perfect and unified cloud-edge-terminal collaborative intelligent system architecture guarantees for the further intelligence of AI algorithms.
- <span id="page-15-3"></span>• *Higher real-time performance*. As more and more sensors are deployed on network terminals, an astonishing amount of real-time data will be generated every second. Since end users and cloud computing centers often need to go through multi-level networks, if the acquired information is uploaded to the cloud, the endto-end latency is difficult to satisfy the performance requirements of applications such as virtual reality and autonomous driving. By sinking computing power from the cloud to the network edge, cloud-edge-terminal intelligent collaboration can complete data processing with low latency which can ensure real-time data analysis.
- *Stronger privacy protection*. Privacy protection has become one of the hottest topics recently as people become more aware of the importance of preventing public data breaches. In traditional cloud computing, users must upload all data to the cloud for encryption. Cloud-edge collaborative intelligence ensures that the original data can be processed at the network edge, thus avoiding the risk of user privacy exposure.

Next, we mainly introduce and summarize five aspects of the cloud-edge-terminal collaboration network combining AI technologies, especially DRL and MADRL methods. The five aspects include task offloading, resource allocation, caching, mobility management, and intelligent emerging applications.

## <span id="page-15-0"></span>V. TASK OFFLOADING AND RESOURCE ALLOCATION FOR AI-ENABLED CLOUD-EDGE-TERMINAL COLLABORATIVE NETWORK

Task offloading and resource allocation techniques are promising methods used in the CETCN that can significant improve the efficiency of task offloading and reduce the task execution delay. This is beneficial for meeting the growing performance requirements of emerging applications, such as low latency and high reliability. In addition, SARL and MARL can provide a good solution for task offloading and resource allocation issues due to the complex and decentralized nature of the CETCN. Hence, in this section, we firstly introduce the models of task offloading and resource allocation for emerging applications in Sections [V-A](#page-15-2) and [V-B.](#page-16-0) These models provide the background for abstracting various operations into optimization problems. Then, we review the works that have been done in task offloading and resource allocation domain in Section [V-C.](#page-17-0) Finally, we point out the possible research directions on task offloading and resource allocation issues for the CETCN in the future in Section [V-D.](#page-24-1)

## <span id="page-15-5"></span><span id="page-15-2"></span>*A. Classification of Task Offloading Models*

In this subsection, we introduce two popular task offloading models, corresponding to binary offloading and partial offloading respectively. A toy example can be seen in Fig. [7.](#page-16-1)

<span id="page-16-2"></span>

| Computing entity | advantage                                                                                                                                   | disadvantage                                                                                                  |
|------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| terminal device  | <ul><li>(1) provide extermely low transmission latency</li><li>(2) solve the congestion of the core network</li></ul>                       | (1) the computing capacity and battery life is limited                                                        |
| edge server      | <ul><li>(1) provide low transmission latency and abundant bandwidth resource</li><li>(2) solve the congestion of the core network</li></ul> | (1) the computing and storage capacity is limited (2) randomness of geographical distribution of edge devices |
| cloud server     | (1) powerful computing and srorage capacity                                                                                                 | (2) high transmission latency                                                                                 |

TABLE III
THE ADVANTAGES AND DISADVANTAGES OF DIFFERENT COMPUTING ENTITIES

![](_page_16_Picture_4.jpeg)

Fig. 7. A toy example of task offloading. The architecture of task offloading is composed of terminal device, base station, edge servers and cloud server. The terminal device uploads the generated tasks to the edge server through the wireless channel. Then the edge server chooses whether to process the task at the edge server or process the task at the cloud server according to the offloading mode of the task.

- <span id="page-16-1"></span>1) Task Model for Binary Offloading: Binary offloading refers to that the task cannot be divided into multiple parts and only can be processed at terminal devices, edge servers or cloud server as a whole. This task offloading model is suited for highly integrated and simple tasks. Such tasks can be represented by a four-tuple  $\{s, f, \tau, c\}$ , where s represents the size of the task (in bits), f represents the CPU cycles required for processing one bit task,  $\tau$  represents the time constraint for processing the task (in seconds), c is the storage space required for a typical task. The four parameters can not only capture the basic properties of various tasks, but also can evaluate the time delay and energy consumption [196]. In general, there are more than one edge server at the edge layer, hence, we can use  $x_{ij}$  to denote the task i which processed at the jth edge server. Similarly, we can use  $x_{ic}$  to denote whether the task i processed at the cloud server and  $x_{il}$  to denote the task that processed at the terminal devices. In the binary offloading mode,  $x_{il}, x_{ij}, x_{ic}$  must satisfy  $x_{il}, x_{ij}, x_{ic} \in \{0, 1\}$  and  $x_l + \sum_{j=1}^{J} x_{ij} + x_{ic} = 1$ , where J denotes the amount of edge
- 2) Task Model for Partial Offloading: In the contrary, if the task can be divided into multiple parts, and different part can be processed at different devices, this task offloading model is refer to partial offloading. Similarly, in the partial offloading mode,  $x_{ij}, x_{ic}$  must satisfy  $x_{il}, x_{ij}, x_{ic} \in [0,1]$  and  $x_{il} + \sum_{j=1}^{J} x_{ij} + x_{ic} = 1$ .  $x_{il} = 0$  /  $x_{ij} = 0$  /  $x_{ic} = 0$  refers to

the situation which terminal device / jth edge server / cloud server do not process the ith task.

Although terminal devices, edge servers, and cloud servers can process tasks generated by different users, these three entities have their own advantages and disadvantages in processing tasks. We summarize the advantages and disadvantages in the Table III.

#### <span id="page-16-0"></span>B. Classification of Resource Allocation Models

The joint computing-communication resource allocation plays a vital role in realizing delay-sensitive and energyefficient CETCN.

1) Computing Model for Task Offloading: The CPU is the core computing component of various computing entities. In general, we adopt CPU-cycle frequency  $f_m$  to evaluate the performance of CPU. The advanced CPU architecture adopts the DVFS technique which can adjust the CPU cycle frequency (voltage), which usually has a maximum value  $f_m^{max}$ . Based on the computing model introduced in Section V-A, the execution time for a particular task  $\{s,f,\tau,c\}$  can be calculated as follows:

<span id="page-16-3"></span>
$$t_m = \frac{sf}{f_m} \tag{1}$$

<span id="page-16-6"></span>The energy consumption is another critical aspect of resource allocation scheme according to the circuit theory [197], [198], [199], [200]. In particular, the authors in [199] have demonstrated that the power consumption is related to the production of  $V_{cir}^2f_m$ , where  $V_{cir}$  is the voltage supplied by circuit. Furthermore, in [197], [200], the clock frequency of CPU chip is approximately linear to the voltage. Thus, the energy consumption is given by  $\kappa f_m^2$ , where  $\kappa$  is the constant. From the above analysis, for a typical task  $\{s,f,\tau,c\}$ , the energy consumption can be represented by

<span id="page-16-4"></span>
$$E_m = \kappa s f f_m^2 \tag{2}$$

<span id="page-16-5"></span>When we adopt partial offloading model, the task handled by different computing entity is decided by  $x_{im}$ , where  $x_{im}$  is referred to  $x_{il}$ ,  $x_{ij}$ ,  $x_{ic}$ . For example, if part of the task handled by jth edge server is  $x_{ij}$ , then the energy consumption can be calculated by  $\kappa x_{ij} sff_j^2$ , where  $f_j$  denotes the CPU-cycle frequency of jth edge server. One can observe from (1) and (2), when allocate computing resources, we need to consider the time deadline and the energy consumption.

2) Communication Model for Task Offloading: In CETCN, an efficient communication resource allocation scheme is

<span id="page-17-1"></span>

TABLE IV CHARACTERISTICS OF TYPICAL WIRELESS COMMUNICATION TECHNOLOGIES AND 6G PERFORMANCE REQUIREMENTS

critic to delay-sensitive applications. To be specific, the wireless channel is different from wired ones in the following aspects [\[201\]](#page-56-33):

- <span id="page-17-2"></span>• Due to reflection and refraction from buildings, walls, and trees, wireless signals face multipath fading during propagation, which makes the channel height time-varying and lead to heavy interference.
- The broadcast nature of wireless transmission causes interference between different signals resulting in a probability of error in detection. To tackle this problem, interference management has become one of the most active research directions in wireless communication systems [\[202\]](#page-56-34), [\[203\]](#page-56-35), [\[204\]](#page-56-36).
- <span id="page-17-5"></span><span id="page-17-3"></span>• Spectrum scarcity has been a major problem for highspeed radio access, which has prompted the design of new transceiver architectures [\[205\]](#page-56-37), [\[206\]](#page-56-38) and network architectures [\[207\]](#page-56-39), [\[208\]](#page-56-40) to improve spectral efficiency. At the same time, the development of spectrum sharing and aggregation technologies [\[209\]](#page-56-41), [\[210\]](#page-56-42), [\[211\]](#page-56-43) is also conducive to promoting the effective use of fragmented and under utilized spectrum resources.

The uncertainty of wireless channels is a critical factor in designing efficient resource allocation schemes in CETCN. For example, when the wireless channel is in deep fading, the delay of task processing reduced by the powerful computing power may not be enough to compensate the increased transmission delay generated by wireless channel. Furthermore, increasing data transmission rate through improving the transmission power may lead to high energy consumption.

Nowadays, various commercialized technologies for modern mobile communication systems have emerged, including *nearfield communication* (NFC), *radio frequency identification* (RFID), Bluetooth, WiFi and 5G. These technologies can support task offloading from terminal devices to edge layer or cloud layer.

<span id="page-17-7"></span>Furthermore, in addition to the huge improvement in maximum coverage, operation frequency and data rate of 6G, the IMT2030 promotion group said that the air port delay of the sixth generation mobile communication system would reach. 1 to 1 millisecond, supporting high-speed mobile communication, the spectrum efficiency will be 1.5 to 3 times higher than 5G, and the energy efficiency will be 20 times higher than 5G. The 6G requirements are also pointed out in terms of maximun coverage, operation frequency and data rate in [\[212\]](#page-57-0). We list the existing communication technologies and 6G requirements in Table [IV.](#page-17-1)

*3) Caching Model for Task Offloading:* On the one hand, the storage capacity of terminal devices and edge servers are limited, on the other hand, most of the traffic on the network is generated by the repeated requests for popular content and applications. Hence, designing reasonable caching placement scheme can efficiently realise the traffic pressure of the core network and reduce the delay of content acquisition [\[213\]](#page-57-1), [\[214\]](#page-57-2), [\[215\]](#page-57-3). Detailed analysis of cooperative caching can be found in Section [VI.](#page-24-0)

<span id="page-17-8"></span>Future mobile communication systems will carry a large number of emerging services, such as AR/VR and driverless driving which require restrict delay guarantee. Therefore, only using edge servers or cloud servers cannot meet the delay requirements of such applications. Besides, advanced communication technologies will further improve the QoS of users.

<span id="page-17-6"></span><span id="page-17-4"></span>From another perspective, due to the heterogeneity of tasks (different tasks have different requirements for delay and bandwidth) and the complexity of edge environments (such as interference between wireless channels), it is difficult to obtain the accurate system models for task offloading which is required for traditional methods. But lucky, the development of DRL can effectively solve the problem. In the following, we will summarize the recent progress of using DRL to solve task offloading in CETCN.

## <span id="page-17-0"></span>*C. Literature Review*

From the user's perspective, a key benefit of CETCN is that it can leverage the capabilities of edge devices for computation offloading, thereby speeding up the computation process and saving energy. Another important aspect of computing offloading is how to use the limited resources at the edge to make the optimal task offloading decisions, such as how to use the available bandwidth, the size of the data volume that needs to be offloaded, and the user's latency requirements to determine how to offload. When tasks are offloaded to CETCN, it is necessary to properly allocate computing resources. Since edge computing nodes are numerous and scattered, we investigate papers that discuss how to efficiently allocate resources to applications that need to be offloaded.

Plenty of efforts have been put into task offloading and resource allocation, such as in [\[39\]](#page-53-11), authors provided a systematic review of task offloading in edge computing scenarios. Because the dimension of the parameters of the problem is very high, the traditional optimization schemes are often

limited. Hence, machine learning algorithms can reduce the difficulty of solving the optimization problem by finding the approximate optimal solution. Therefore, in [\[45\]](#page-53-17), the author made a review of the application of machine learning in the MEC system and pointed out the problems faced by machine learning in solving these issues. Based on different offloading schemes and different optimization objectives, such as delay minimization and energy efficiency maximization, different optimization problems can be obtained. Due to the challenges faced by CETCN, DRL becomes a promising method in solving these issues. Therefore, in this section, we review the papers that utilize the DRL to solve task offloading and resource allocation issues in CETCN in recent years. We summarize this section in Tables [V](#page-18-0) and [VI](#page-19-0) respectively.

<span id="page-18-1"></span>*1) Task Offloading:* In [\[216\]](#page-57-4), the authors study the realization of an efficient multi-user distributed offloading mechanism in a time-varying channel. First, with the help of game theory, the edge nodes need to determine the task offloading decisions. Second, the author extends the problem to scenarios where the channel information is uncertain and proposes a RL-based offloading mechanism to help edge nodes learn optimal offloading strategies. Wang et al. [\[217\]](#page-57-5) discuss the task offloading problem in MEC system. In the small cell scenarios, the computing tasks requested by the user are divided into computing tasks performed by the user and the MEC server, tasks performed by the MEC server alone, and tasks performed by the users themselves. Due to the different data sizes of tasks requested by users, the authors describe the problem as a transmission and calculation delays minimization problem and propose a multi-stack Qlearning method to improve learning efficiency. To address the stringent requirements of MEC systems for offloading scalability, [\[218\]](#page-57-6) presents a collaborative computing system consisting of mobile devices, edge clouds, and central clouds. Based on Q-learning, a computational offloading strategy is designed, and the numerical results show that the scheme can effectively improve the user's service experience.

<span id="page-18-4"></span><span id="page-18-3"></span><span id="page-18-2"></span>In [\[219\]](#page-57-7), in order to minimize the energy consumption in mobile Internet, the authors formulate a power minimization problem and propose a novel DRL based green task offloading scheme in end-edge-cloud collaborative network to make task offloading decisions, extensive experimental results indicate the proposed scheme is superior to other baseline solutions. The movement of terminal devices poses difficulties for cloud edge collaborative task offloading. At the same time, maintaining continuous migration of mobile device's tasks is
